Problem sheet 6 · Coherence and the confinement spectrum

Reconstruct a two-state density matrix, identify a degeneracy splitting and test a geometric inference from high-energy levels.

Tomography of an imperfect preparation

For independently repeated preparations the probabilities for positive Pauli \(x,y,z\) outcomes are \(0.70,0.40,0.60\). Determine the Bloch vector, density matrix and purity. What would dephasing in the \(z\) basis do to each probability at long times?

Show the reconstruction

Each Bloch component is \(2P_+-1\), giving \((0.40,-0.20,0.20)\). From \(\rho=(I+\mathbf r\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma)/2\), diagonal entries are \(0.60,0.40\) and the upper off-diagonal entry is \((r_x-ir_y)/2=0.20+0.10i\). Purity is \((1+|\mathbf r|^2)/2=(1+0.24)/2=0.62\). Pure \(z\) dephasing removes the two transverse components while preserving populations, so the final positive probabilities are \(0.50,0.50,0.60\). The nonzero \(y\) component would be missed by measuring only one transverse basis.

Split a square-well doublet

For an electron in a 3.00 nm square well compare the \((2,1)\) and \((1,2)\) energies. Stretch the \(x\) side to 3.30 nm while holding \(y\) fixed and compute both levels. Take \(\hbar^2/(2m_e)=0.03810\) eV nm².

Show the directional energy shifts

In the square both have \(E=0.03810\pi^2(5/9)=0.209\) eV. After stretching, the \((2,1)\) level has \(E=0.03810\pi^2(4/3.30^2+1/3.00^2)=0.180\) eV. For \((1,2)\) the factor is \(1/3.30^2+4/3.00^2\), yielding \(0.202\) eV. Modes with more rapid oscillations along the stretched axis lose more gradient energy. Uniform scaling of both dimensions would preserve the degeneracy.

Estimate the area from a level count

A spinless two-dimensional Dirichlet well has about 2000 levels up to \(k=50.0\) nm⁻¹. Use the leading counting law to estimate area. If an independent perimeter measurement gives 11.5 nm, apply the first boundary correction and discuss the precision of the inferred shape.

Show the asymptotic inversion

Using \(N\simeq Ak^2/(4\pi)\) gives \(A\simeq4\pi(2000)/2500=10.05\) nm². With \(N\simeq Ak^2/(4\pi)-Pk/(4\pi)\) the inferred area increases by \(P/k=0.230\) nm² to about \(10.28\) nm². Low-order terms and fluctuations from integer level counts limit precision at finite \(k\). Neither area nor perimeter fixes the full boundary, and even distinct shapes with identical spectra are possible. The estimate uses each spatial state once; including a separate spin degeneracy would double a counted list.

Recover a state from three spin measurements

Three independently prepared ensembles give probabilities for spin-up of 0.80 along x, 0.50 along y and 0.90 along z. Reconstruct the density matrix, decide whether the state is pure, and predict the result of an x measurement after a z measurement whose result is discarded. Repeat the purity check if the z probability was 0.95.

Worked solution

For each Pauli measurement the mean is p(+) − p(−) = 2p(+) − 1. The Bloch vector is (0.6, 0, 0.8). In the z basis the diagonal entries are 0.9 and 0.1 and both off-diagonal entries equal 0.3. Its trace is one and its determinant vanishes. Thus its eigenvalues are one and zero. A state vector is (√0.9, √0.1) up to global phase.

An unread projective z measurement removes the off-diagonal terms. The z probabilities stay fixed, while the subsequent x-up probability becomes 0.50. The new purity is 0.9² + 0.1² = 0.82. Selecting the z-up subset instead would leave a pure z-up state, which also gives 0.50 in x. Testing z again distinguishes selection from mixing.

With z-up probability 0.95 the reconstructed Bloch length squared would become 0.6² + 0.9² = 1.17. The nominal matrix has a negative eigenvalue. Finite-sample fluctuations or calibration differences can produce such estimates. A physical reconstruction fits a positive matrix to the original counts. The three quoted probabilities alone do not specify their statistical uncertainty.

Which energy change really establishes a length change?

The numerical polygon gives a ground energy of 0.09670 eV for an electron with a 1 nm length unit. A second device yields 0.04835 eV and the same dimensionless ground eigenvalue. Infer its length if the mass is unchanged. Construct a different explanation with unchanged geometry. Would measuring the second energy level distinguish the two explanations for a uniform effective mass?

Work through the solution

The hard-wall problem gives \(E_n=\hbar^2\lambda_n/(2m\ell^2)\). A factor of two drop at fixed mass requires \(\ell_2/\ell_1=\sqrt{2}\), or 1.414 nm. At the original scale, doubling the effective mass produces the same ground energy. Both mechanisms multiply every energy by one half under the stated assumptions. A second eigenvalue ratio therefore cannot resolve this ambiguity. An independent length or mass measurement can.

Use the OneSimulation laboratory to check your answer

Chapter 13 · When a free quantum packet initially gets narrower

A position distribution and a momentum distribution alone do not determine the early motion of a wave packet. Reconstruct free expansion from their covariance and compare two preparations with the same widths.

Ask for a width, rather than a single trajectory

Prepare many identical particles, release them in free space and measure one position from each preparation at a chosen delay. The spread in that collection of positions is the observable \(\sigma_x(t)\). A particle detected early cannot also contribute an undisturbed late-time position to the same release. Quantum expectations below describe an ensemble of repeated preparations.

For a free particle in one dimension \(H=p^2/(2m)\). In the Heisenberg picture the preparation stays fixed while the operators carry the time dependence. The identity \([AB,C]=A[B,C]+[A,C]B\) and \([x,p]=i\hbar\) give

\[\dot p=(i/\hbar)[H,p]=0,\]
\[\dot x=(i/\hbar)[H,x]=p/m,\]
\[x(t)=x(0)+tp(0)/m .\]

Subtract the expectation value on both sides. Squaring this centred operator requires both multiplication orders for its cross term because \(x\) and \(p\) do not commute. Set \(C=(\langle\Delta x\Delta p+\Delta p\Delta x\rangle)/2\), a real number with units of action. The exact result for any free preparation with these finite moments is

\[\sigma_x^2(t)=\sigma_x^2(0)+\frac{2t}{m}C+\frac{t^2}{m^2}\sigma_p^2(0).\]

Give a Gaussian a spatially varying phase

Let the initial wavefunction be proportional to \(\exp[-x^2/(4s^2)+ibx^2/(4s^2)]\), with \(s>0\). Its density is a Gaussian of variance \(s^2\) for every real \(b\). Act with \(-i\hbar\,\partial_x\) before taking moments. The derivative gives \(p\psi=\hbar(b+i)x\psi/(2s^2)\). Consequently \(\langle p\rangle=0\), \(\sigma_p^2=\|p\psi\|^2=\hbar^2(1+b^2)/(4s^2)\), and \(C=\hbar b/2\), the real part of \(\langle xp\rangle\). For negative \(b\), probability on the right initially flows left and probability on the left flows right.

\[\frac{\sigma_x^2(t)}{s^2}=1+2b\,u+(1+b^2)u^2,\]
\[u=\frac{\hbar t}{2ms^2}.\]

Stationarity of the quadratic gives \(u_*=-b/(1+b^2)\). A future focus occurs for negative \(b\), and its width is \(s/\sqrt{1+b^2}\). The preparation saturates \(\sigma_x^2\sigma_p^2-C^2=\hbar^2/4\), so a position focus does not violate uncertainty. It removes the initial position–momentum correlation and leaves the same large momentum spread.

Predict two releases

Take \(b=-2\) and \(s=4.0\) µm. At \(u=0.40\) the width falls to \(4/\sqrt5=1.79\) µm. With \(b=+2\), both initial marginal widths are unchanged, but at the same time the packet has width \(4\sqrt{3.4}=7.38\) µm. An initial image cannot distinguish the two releases. Measuring a second image at known positive delay resolves the sign.

For a third preparation use \(b=0\). This one starts with the smallest compatible momentum spread for the specified \(s\) and reaches \(s\sqrt2\) at \(u=1\). In all three cases the mean stays at the origin. Translating the packet or giving it a common mean momentum would translate that mean trajectory without changing the variance formula.

Find the focus

Adjust the phase curvature. The plot compares the chosen preparation with an unchirped Gaussian of the same starting position width. Locate the focus with the derivative of the variance before reading its time from the result.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on a missing phase

A Gaussian initially 3.0 µm wide focuses to 1.5 µm after release. Find its initial chirp and dimensionless focusing time.

Work through the solution

The ratio \(s/s_{\min}=2\) gives \(1+b^2=4\). A future focus selects \(b=-\sqrt3\). Hence \(u_*=\sqrt3/4=0.4330\). A packet with the opposite sign would have reached that same minimum before the stated release time under free evolution.

Two detectors measure packet widths at scaled delays \(u=+0.20\) and \(u=-0.20\) relative to a chosen reference preparation. Their squared widths differ by \(-25.6\,\mu\mathrm m^2\), with \(s=4\,\mu\mathrm m\). Infer \(b\) from just this difference.

Work through the solution

Subtracting the width-squared formula at opposite delays cancels the constant and quadratic terms, giving \(4s^2bu\). Hence \(b=-25.6/(4\times16\times0.20)=-2\). The sign comes entirely from the position–momentum covariance. Choosing the reference preparation partway through free expansion makes measurements on both sides physically meaningful.

Can a non-Gaussian free state with finite second moments initially contract? What minimum variance follows from the general expression?

Work through the solution

Differentiate the moment formula to get \(t_*=-mC/\sigma_p^2\). A future minimum requires \(C<0\). Its variance is \(\sigma_x^2(0)-C^2/\sigma_p^2\), bounded below by \(\hbar^2/(4\sigma_p^2)\) using the Schrödinger–Robertson inequality. The Gaussian in this section attains the bound, but initial contraction requires only a negative covariance.

Further reading · Fließbach, Schrödingers Wellenmechanik · Tong, Quantum Mechanics, wavefunctions and time evolution.

Chapter 14 · Boundary conditions that carry a current around a ring

An electron on an ideal narrow ring can sustain current in a stationary state. Work out how the topology of its configuration space and a magnetic flux through the hole select the allowed currents.

Recover the allowed state space from a boundary term

Wrap a coordinate \(s\) of length \(L\) around the ring. The points \(s=0\) and \(s=L\) label the same physical place. For a uniform one-dimensional kinetic operator, integration by parts shows that the difference between \(\langle\phi,H\psi\rangle\) and \(\langle H\phi,\psi\rangle\) is proportional to

\[[\,\phi^*\partial_s\psi-(\partial_s\phi)^*\psi\,]_{0}^{L}.\]

Periodic values and periodic first derivatives make this expression vanish for any two admissible states. They also make the probability current continuous at the join. A plane wave \(e^{iks}/\sqrt L\) then requires \(kL=2\pi n\) with integer \(n\). The labels \(n\) and \(-n\) have equal energy at zero flux but circulate oppositely. A real combination of the pair has zero net current. An energy measurement on its own does not tell us which combination was prepared.

Put flux through the hole

For a particle of charge \(q\), the kinetic momentum along the ring is \(-i\hbar\partial_s-qA_s\). Choose a constant tangential vector potential \(A_s=\Phi/L\) giving the required circulation \(\oint A_s ds=\Phi\). Define signed flux \(\varphi=q\Phi/h\). Periodic plane waves remain eigenfunctions, while their kinetic momenta and energies now change:

\[p_{\rm kin}=\frac{h}{L}(n-\varphi),\]
\[E_n=E_L(n-\varphi)^2,\]
\[E_L=\frac{h^2}{2mL^2}.\]

A gauge transformation can eliminate the constant \(A_s\) locally. It multiplies a wavefunction by a phase whose value after one circuit differs from its initial value by \(e^{-2\pi i\varphi}\) in this gauge. The transformed wavefunction must carry that twist at the join. Imposing both zero vector potential and unchanged periodicity would discard the physical flux dependence. Increasing \(\varphi\) by one reorders the spectrum by a shift of the integer labels.

Work out what a current measurement sees

One particle of velocity \(v=p_{\rm kin}/m\) goes around the ring in a time \(L/|v|\). The signed charge passing a fixed section per unit time is \(I=qv/L\). Differentiating \(E_n\) at fixed \(n\) gives the same result:

\[I_n=\frac{qh}{mL^2}(n-\varphi)=-\frac{\partial E_n}{\partial \Phi}.\]

Take \(\varphi=0.30\). In units of \(E_L\), the \(n=0\) energy is 0.09 and the \(n=1\) energy is 0.49. The currents in units of \(qh/(mL^2)\) are −0.30 and +0.70 respectively. A probe that selects the lower-energy branch here sees a circulating state even though its probability density is uniform around the ring.

At \(\varphi=0.50\) the two energies agree at \(0.25 E_L\). Following a given eigenstate through this crossing and allowing a system to relax into its lowest energy at every flux describe different experiments. An ideal isolated perfectly uniform ring conserves its angular momentum label. Weak imperfections or a reservoir can change the populations and modify the behaviour near degeneracy.

Inspect the neighbouring branches

Change the signed flux continuously. Compare the slope of the branch containing \(n=0\) with its current. Predict where the lowest pair changes ordering and check that the complete set repeats with unit flux.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on a ring of another size

Double the circumference at fixed mass and flux while preparing the same integer state. Predict the changes of its wave number, energy and current.

Work through the solution

The canonical and kinetic wave numbers each halve because the integer label and signed flux are fixed. \(E_L\) scales as \(L^{-2}\), and so does the current unit \(qh/(mL^2)\). Energy and current both fall to one quarter. The flux period \(h/|q|\) stays unchanged.

A ring with fixed flux is enlarged to twice its original circumference without changing the particle mass. How do energy gaps and branch currents change? Does the location of a crossing in flux move?

Work through the solution

\(E_L=h^2/(2mL^2)\) falls by a factor of four. The current scale \(qh/(mL^2)\) falls by the same factor. The signed flux variable contains no circumference when total flux is held fixed, so crossings stay at half-integer flux. Holding the magnetic field fixed as ring area grows would instead change the enclosed flux.

At \(\varphi=0.50\), prepare an incoherent mixture with equal populations on \(n=0\) and \(n=1\). Calculate its current and energy. Can observing zero average current distinguish this mixture from zero flux?

Work through the solution

The currents are opposite, each of magnitude half the current unit, and the mean vanishes. Both energies are \(0.25E_L\). A zero-flux ground state also has zero current, but its energy is zero in the same calibration. Spectroscopy or a change of population separates the two cases.

Further reading · Tong, Particles in Magnetic Fields, gauge transformations and Aharonov–Bohm effect · Fließbach, Quantenmechanik, Eigenwerte und Eigenfunktionen.

Chapter 15 · Why the Coulomb radial series terminates

The hydrogen ground state admits a simple exponential guess. To understand whole spectral series, derive the radial recurrence and follow the two boundary conditions that force quantized energies.

Reduce the relative motion

A Coulomb attraction \(V=-\kappa/r\) depends only on the separation of the electron and the positive centre. Separating the centre of mass leaves the reduced mass \(\mu\). Write the relative wavefunction as \(u(r)Y_{\ell m}(\theta,\phi)/r\), with a normalized angular function. The \(r^2\) in the volume element cancels the inverse square of the radial denominator, so normalization is \(\int_0^\infty |u|^2 dr=1\). The radial equation is

\[-\frac{\hbar^2}{2\mu}u''+\left[\frac{\hbar^2\ell(\ell+1)}{2\mu r^2}-\frac{\kappa}{r}\right]u=Eu .\]

For a bound state define \(k=\sqrt{-2\mu E}/\hbar\) and \(\rho=2kr\). At large radius the constant binding energy gives a decaying factor \(e^{-\rho/2}\). At small radius the centrifugal term yields powers \(\rho^{\ell+1}\) and \(\rho^{-\ell}\). The regular Coulomb problem selects the first power. For \(\ell=0\) this enforces the regular three-dimensional boundary condition \(u(0)=0\). These two endpoint behaviours suggest factoring \(u=\rho^{\ell+1}e^{-\rho/2}v(\rho)\).

Let normalizability select the energy

Differentiate the product twice and cancel the constant and inverse-square terms with the radial equation. The remaining polynomial equation involves only the dimensionless attraction \(\lambda=\mu\kappa/(\hbar^2 k)\):

\[\rho v''+(2\ell+2-\rho)v'+(\lambda-\ell-1)v=0 .\]

Insert \(v=\sum_{j=0}^\infty a_j\rho^j\) and equate equal powers. The derivative term proportional to \(a_{j+1}\) contains both the factor \(j+1\) and the angular factor \(j+2\ell+2\).

\[a_{j+1}=\frac{j+\ell+1-\lambda}{(j+1)(j+2\ell+2)}a_j .\]

A generic regular series has large-order coefficient ratio approaching \(1/j\); its growing exponential component eventually overwhelms the front factor \(e^{-\rho/2}\). For the regular Coulomb solution the coefficient of that growing component vanishes when the series terminates. A nonnegative integer \(n_r\) makes the numerator zero at \(j=n_r\). Thus \(\lambda=n_r+\ell+1=n\), and

\[E_n=-\frac{\mu\kappa^2}{2\hbar^2n^2}.\]

Here \(n_r\) counts radial nodes in the open interval. Angular nodes and radial nodes enter the same energy label in this particular potential. Spin, fine structure, finite nuclear size and external fields are absent from this calculation.

Distinguish two equal energies spatially

For \(n=2,\ell=0\), the recurrence gives \(a_1/a_0=-1/2\). With \(a=\hbar^2/(\mu\kappa)\), this state has \(\rho=r/a\), so its radial probability vanishes at \(r=2a\). For \(n=2,\ell=1\), the numerator vanishes already at \(j=0\). Here the radial probability is proportional to \(r^4e^{-r/a}\), with a maximum at \(4a\) and no internal radial zero. The two preparations can yield the same spectroscopic energy but different radial histograms.

For an ideal hydrogenic ion with nuclear charge twice as large and the same reduced mass, \(\kappa\) doubles and \(a\) halves. Every radial feature just discussed moves to half its old radius. Each binding energy magnitude increases by four. For a Rydberg energy of 13.6 eV at charge one, the \(n=3\) to \(n=2\) photon has energy \(13.6(1/4-1/9)=1.89\) eV, rising to 7.56 eV for charge two.

Compare radial probabilities

Change the Coulomb strength while keeping the plotting unit equal to the original Bohr radius. The curves are separately normalized radial probabilities per unit of that original length. Locate the moving 2s node and the maximum of the 2p curve.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on a third radial state

For \(n=3,\ell=1\), obtain the first coefficient ratio, the radial node and its radius in units of \(a\).

Work through the solution

The recurrence at \(j=0\) gives \((2-3)/(2+2)=-1/4\). The next numerator vanishes, so \(v=a_0(1-\rho/4)\). The node is \(\rho=4\). Because \(k=1/(3a)\), the physical radius is \(r=\rho/(2k)=6a\).

Compute the ratio of the most probable 2p radius to its expectation value. Why can those two characteristic radii differ for the same orbital?

Work through the solution

With \(x=Zr/a_0\) the radial density is \(x^4e^{-x}/24\). Its derivative vanishes at \(x=4\), whereas the mean is \(\int_0^\infty x^5e^{-x}dx/24=5\). Thus the ratio is \(4/5\). The long tail shifts the mean above the mode. A local spatial probability density would be a different distribution again because it lacks the integrated spherical shell factor.

For an ideal Coulomb centre with \(Z=2\), predict the ratio of the \(n=3\) to \(n=2\) binding energies and the photon energy released from level three to level two in units of the hydrogen Rydberg. Neglect changes in reduced mass.

Work through the solution

Binding magnitudes are \(Z^2\mathrm{Ry}/n^2\). Their ratio is \(4/9\), and the positive emitted energy is \(4\mathrm{Ry}(1/4-1/9)=5\mathrm{Ry}/9\). The allowed orbital changes would also need to satisfy a radiative selection rule before an actual emission line appears.

Further reading · Tong, A Quantum Particle in Three Dimensions · Fließbach, Quantenmechanik, Dreidimensionale Probleme.

Chapter 16 · Measure an interaction by its low-energy scattering length

A cloud of cold, distinguishable atoms can scatter as if it had a size much greater than the range of its mutual force. Derive that length by matching a radial wave on the two sides of a simple potential well.

Replace a trajectory by a flux

For a localized central interaction an incident plane wave at large distance can be written as the sum of its incoming state and an outgoing spherical wave of amplitude \(f(\theta)\). The spherical-wave intensity falls as \(1/r^2\). Multiplying its radial probability current by the detector area \(r^2d\Omega\) and dividing by the incident current leaves \(d\sigma/d\Omega=|f|^2\). This ratio is a geometrical area per unit solid angle even though the motion does not supply a definite classical impact parameter.

A partial wave with angular momentum \(\ell\) feels the centrifugal term \(\hbar^2\ell(\ell+1)/(2\mu r^2)\). When \(kR\ll1\) for an interaction of range \(R\), higher partial waves usually reach the interior only weakly. In the absence of higher-wave resonances keep the \(\ell=0\) wave. Far outside, its radial part is proportional to \(\sin(kr+\delta_0)\). The phase shift measures how the interaction changes the oscillation relative to the free solution.

Match the zero-energy solutions

Choose \(V=-V_0\) for \(r<R\) and zero outside. At zero collision energy the exterior radial equation is \(u''=0\), hence \(u_{\rm out}=A(r-a_s)\). The extrapolated intercept \(a_s\) defines the scattering length. Inside the well the regular solution is \(B\sin(Kr)\), \(K=\sqrt{2\mu V_0}/\hbar\). Continuity of \(u,u'\) at \(R\) allows their amplitude to cancel from the logarithmic derivative:

\[K\cot(KR)=\frac{1}{R-a_s},\]
\[\frac{a_s}{R}=1-\frac{\tan x}{x},\]
\[x=KR .\]

In the weak-well limit \(\tan x=x+x^3/3+\cdots\), so \(a_s\simeq-Rx^2/3\). At \(x=\pi/2\) a state reaches the bound-state threshold; the exterior zero-energy slope vanishes relative to its boundary value. The extrapolated intercept diverges. Its sign on either side of this crossing records the phase of the wave, rather than the sign of a physical radius.

Keep the finite-energy bound on the cross section

Expanding \(\sin(kr+\delta_0)\) at small \(k\) gives \(k\cot\delta_0\simeq-1/a_s\), neglecting effective-range terms. The outgoing-wave amplitude and the integrated cross section of the isotropic component are

\[f_0=\frac{1}{k\cot\delta_0-ik},\]
\[\sigma_0\simeq\frac{4\pi a_s^2}{1+k^2a_s^2}.\]

Far from resonance with \(|ka_s|\ll1\), \(\sigma_0\simeq4\pi a_s^2\). At fixed nonzero \(k\), increasing \(|a_s|\) instead takes \(\sigma_0\) towards \(4\pi/k^2\). Keeping the imaginary term from outgoing flux is essential in this regime. A finite range adds terms of order \(k^2\) to the real denominator. Identical atoms require exchange symmetrization and can have different allowed partial waves.

For \(x=\pi/4\), \(a_s/R=1-4/\pi=-0.2732\). With \(kR=0.10\) the cross section is about \(0.937R^2\). At \(x=\pi\), \(a_s=R\), and the same incoming wave gives \(12.44R^2\). Thus increasing attraction does not produce a monotone core size. It changes a phase accumulated inside the well.

Change the depth of the well

The regularized cross-section curve stays finite across the first threshold. Vary \(kR\) to see how wavelength sets the height and breadth of the peak. Compare the selected depth with its two weak-scattering limits.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on an ambiguous area

A low-energy measurement at \(kR=0.10\) finds \(\sigma=2\pi R^2\). Infer \(|a_s|/R\) and identify what measurement is still missing.

Work through the solution

Writing \(b=|a_s|/R\), the equation \(2\pi=4\pi b^2/(1+0.01b^2)\) gives \(b=1/\sqrt{1.99}=0.709\). The sign disappears in this cross section. A measurement sensitive to the scattering phase, or independent information about a near-threshold bound state, is needed to determine it.

A measured low-energy cross section equals half the unitarity value \(4\pi/k^2\) at nonzero \(k\). Infer the magnitude of the scattering length. Can this observation establish which side of a resonance the depth lies on?

Work through the solution

Set \(k^2a_s^2/(1+k^2a_s^2)=1/2\), giving \(|a_s|=1/k\). The total cross section depends on \(a_s^2\) at this order. A phase-sensitive measurement of the scattering amplitude or independent knowledge of a bound state can resolve its sign. This inversion also presumes effective-range corrections are small.

Obtain the leading scattering length for a shallow well and predict how the zero-energy cross section changes when the well depth doubles.

Work through the solution

Expand \(\tan x=x+x^3/3+\cdots\), giving \(a_s/R\simeq-x^2/3\). Since \(x^2=2mV_0R^2/\hbar^2\), the scattering length scales linearly and negatively with well depth. In the weak-scattering limit \(\sigma\simeq4\pi a_s^2\), so doubling a sufficiently shallow depth quadruples the cross section.

Further reading · Tong, Applications of Quantum Mechanics, Scattering Theory.

Chapter 17 · A phase accumulated by slowly turning a magnetic field

A spin aligned with a slowly rotating field returns to its original measurement probabilities after one loop. Derive the additional phase that becomes observable when that path interferes with a reference path.

Separate following the state from acquiring a phase

Let a spin one-half have \(H=-(\hbar\omega_0/2)\,\mathbf n(t)\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma\), with \(\mathbf n\) a unit vector and constant \(\omega_0>0\). The two instantaneous energies are separated by \(\hbar\omega_0\). Prepare the lower-energy state and rotate \(\mathbf n\) on a smooth path. If the rate of change of its eigenvector is small compared with the gap in angular-frequency units, transitions to the upper state are small. Following one eigenstate still allows a time-dependent complex phase.

Write \(|\psi(t)\rangle=e^{i\alpha(t)}|n(t)\rangle\), substitute in the time-dependent Schrödinger equation and project onto \(\langle n|\). Normalization makes \(\langle n|\dot n\rangle\) imaginary. Consequently

\[\dot\alpha=-E_n/\hbar+i\langle n|\dot n\rangle .\]

The first contribution accumulates in proportion to elapsed time. The integral of the second depends on the path of the instantaneous eigenvector. The splitting into pieces changes with the arbitrary phase used to define \(|n(t)\rangle\); after a closed loop the geometric phase in a single-valued gauge is invariant modulo \(2\pi\).

Evaluate a circle on the sphere of directions

Represent the aligned spin by the column \((\cos(\theta/2),e^{i\phi}\sin(\theta/2))^T\). Its mean spin points along the polar angles \(\theta,\phi\). Hold \(\theta\) fixed and increase \(\phi\) by one revolution. Only the lower component contributes to the derivative. Its coefficient is its occupation weight:

\[i\langle n|\partial_\phi n\rangle=-\sin^2(\theta/2),\]
\[\gamma=-2\pi\sin^2(\theta/2)=-\pi(1-\cos\theta).\]

The solid angle enclosed by the latitude is \(2\pi(1-\cos\theta)\), found by integrating \(\sin\theta'\,d\theta'\,d\phi\) over the cap. The aligned spin accumulates minus half this angle. Reversing traversal changes its sign. As the cone closes to a point, the result vanishes continuously. Around the equator the result is \(-\pi\).

Predict an interference fringe

Split a coherent preparation into two equal-amplitude paths and arrange the reference so the dynamical phases match. If only the signal path gains \(\gamma\), an ideal balanced recombiner gives the bright-port probability \(P=(1+\cos\gamma)/2\) for zero additional reference phase. The overall phase of one isolated spin would never produce this signal. The reference path supplies a phase comparison.

For \(\theta=60^\circ\), the solid angle is \(\pi\), the geometric phase is \(-\pi/2\), and \(P=1/2\). For a circuit of the equator the same port becomes dark. Reversing the \(60^\circ\) loop still gives one-half at zero reference phase. Introduce a known reference shift \(\chi\): the probability becomes \([1+\cos(\chi+\gamma)]/2\) in the chosen convention. Choosing \(\chi=\pi/2\) changes the two directions of traversal into full and zero probability, revealing the sign.

Scan the reference phase

Set the cone angle, then vary the reference phase. The horizontal axis scans the cone angle and the marker value is returned in the readout. A source with finite path coherence would reduce the modulation depth, whereas unmatched dynamical phases would shift the fringe.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on the following condition

A cone has angle \(60^\circ\). At what rate scale does uniform azimuthal rotation stop being reliably adiabatic, and why can a change in loop duration alter a real experiment?

Work through the solution

The off-diagonal eigenstate derivative has magnitude \(|\dot\phi|\sin\theta/2\), so one requires \(|\dot\phi|\sin\theta/(2\omega_0)\ll1\). Increasing duration reduces that coupling but also changes the dynamical phase and can permit decoherence. An interferometer must control both even when the ideal geometric phase has no dependence on duration.

Keep the cone angle at \(60^\circ\) but make two complete positive circuits before recombination. What probability appears at the bright port with zero reference phase? Would performing one forward and one reverse circuit give the same result?

Work through the solution

Each positive traversal contributes \(-\pi/2\). Two give \(-\pi\), hence zero bright-port probability. A positive and negative traversal instead add to zero, giving unit probability when dynamical phases are matched. These outcomes test orientation and addition of the geometric phase over consecutive cycles.

Estimate the sensitivity to a cone-angle calibration error at \(60^\circ\). Express the first-order phase error for one angular degree.

Work through the solution

Differentiating in radians gives \(d\gamma/d\theta=-\pi\sin\theta\). Multiplication by \(\pi/180\) yields \(-0.0475\) radians of phase error per added degree at \(60^\circ\). At a port fringe extremum the intensity change from this small phase error begins quadratically. Near a half-bright fringe it appears linearly and is easier to detect.

Further reading · Tong, Topics in Quantum Mechanics, adiabatic theorem and Berry phase.

Chapter 18 · A sudden expansion and the distribution of measured energy

A particle occupies the lowest state of an infinite square well. One wall is suddenly moved outward. Its position density initially stays where it was, although the list of energies an observer can measure changes immediately. An overlap integral predicts the complete distribution.

Preserve the state and change the basis

Take the old interval \(0<x<L\) and new interval \(0<x<bL\) with \(b>1\). The old state is \(\sqrt{2/L}\sin(\pi x/L)\) inside its well, extended by zero across the newly accessible interval. The sudden approximation means evolution of the state during the control change can be neglected for the modes of interest. Immediately after the change, expand this same function in normalised new modes \(\sqrt{2/(bL)}\sin(n\pi x/(bL))\). The measurement probability for mode \(n\) is the absolute square of its expansion coefficient.

With \(u=x/L\) the coefficient is \(2b^{-1/2}\int_0^1\sin\pi u\sin(n\pi u/b)\,du\). Product-to-sum reduces it to the difference of two cosine integrals. At their upper endpoints \(\sin[\pi(1-n/b)]=\sin(\pi n/b)\) while \(\sin[\pi(1+n/b)]=-\sin(\pi n/b)\). Collecting the denominators gives

\[c_n=\frac{2}{\pi\sqrt b}\frac{\sin(\pi n/b)}{1-(n/b)^2}.\]

When \(n/b=1\) the expression has a removable singularity. The original integral then contains \(\sin^2\pi u\), giving coefficient \(1/\sqrt b\). When \(n/b\) is an integer other than one, orthogonality on the old interval makes the overlap zero. Completeness of the new sine basis requires \(\sum_n|c_n|^2=1\), which gives a numerical truncation check.

Interpret an energy measurement

For \(b=2\), the first two coefficients are \(8/(3\pi\sqrt2)\) and \(1/\sqrt2\). Their probabilities are 0.3603 and 0.5000. Mode three has coefficient \(8/(5\pi\sqrt2)\) and probability 0.1297. A measurement of the new ground energy therefore occurs only about 36 percent of the time. In contrast an adiabatic expansion that remains in the instantaneous nondegenerate ground state produces the new ground energy with probability essentially one.

The new level energies are \(E_n=E_{\rm old}(n/b)^2\). The initial post-quench energy expectation is most safely evaluated as the kinetic quadratic form \((\hbar^2/2m)\int|\psi'|^2dx\). The wavefunction vanishes at the old boundary and its weak first derivative is square-integrable. The integral extends by zero and keeps its pre-expansion value \(E_{\rm old}\). For an instantaneous outward move the energy distribution broadens although this mean stays the same. The derivative jumps at the former wall, so applying the squared new Hamiltonian requires care. Generically \(|c_n|^2=O(n^{-4})\); the mean-energy sum converges, while the second energy moment diverges in this ideal step model.

See which observable converges first

Vary the expansion ratio and number of retained modes. The graph shows individual probabilities and accumulated probability. The readout separately sums the energy-weighted probabilities. A small omitted total probability can carry a larger fraction of the mean energy because higher modes have greater energy per observation. Refine the cutoff until the quantity you intend to compare has converged. A real finite-speed wall or finite confining barrier changes the high-energy tail.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on expansion ratios and missing weight

Triple the box width. What is the probability that the measured post-quench energy equals the old ground energy? Which higher states have precisely zero weight?

Work through the solution

The matching energy requires \(n=3\). Its coefficient is the removable-limit value \(1/\sqrt3\), so probability is one third. States \(n=6,9,12,\ldots\) are orthogonal to the initial mode on the old interval and have zero overlap. Other modes generally have nonzero weight.

Is the original particle certain to be found somewhere in the old interval immediately after a sudden expansion? Is it certain to be there at a later time?

Work through the solution

Initially the state has zero amplitude on the added interval, so the first probability is one. At later times the new energy components acquire unequal phases \(e^{-iE_nt/\hbar}\). Their cancellations outside the old interval need no longer hold. Energy populations stay fixed under the new static Hamiltonian while position probabilities evolve.

Further reading · Cambridge quantum mechanics, sudden approximation and quantum quenches.

Chapter 19 · Adiabatic following and a finite sweep through an avoided crossing

Two unperturbed energies cross as a control rises. A nonzero coupling opens a gap. Whether a prepared particle follows the lower energy through the crossing depends on how quickly the eigenvectors rotate compared with the dynamical phase they accumulate.

Find the gap and the rotation rate

Let \(\hbar=1\) temporarily, with Hamiltonian \(H(t)=\begin{pmatrix}vt/2&g\\g&-vt/2\end{pmatrix}\), \(v>0\), \(g>0\). The fixed basis is called diabatic because each basis vector keeps its identity as the detuning \(vt\) changes sign. Squaring the Hamiltonian gives \([(vt/2)^2+g^2]I\); its energies are \(\pm h(t)\) with \(h=\sqrt{(vt/2)^2+g^2}\). The minimum gap is \(2g\). The corresponding Bloch axis points along \((g,0,vt/2)\); write its polar angle as \(\theta=\operatorname{atan2}(2g,vt)\).

Differentiating the angle gives \(\dot\theta=-2gv/[(vt)^2+4g^2]\). A spinor aligned with this axis rotates through half its geometric angle in Hilbert space. The magnitude of the off-diagonal derivative coupling is \(|\dot\theta|/2\), largest at the crossing where it equals \(v/(4g)\). Comparison with the minimum gap frequency \(2g/\hbar\) gives a local adiabatic ratio \(\hbar v/(8g^2)\). A slow sweep or large coupling suppresses transitions between instantaneous energy states. Following the lower adiabatic state exchanges the two fixed basis labels across the crossing.

Derive the fast-sweep scale independently

In the interaction picture of the diagonal part, the energy difference \(vt\) gives an accumulated relative phase \(vt^2/(2\hbar)\). First-order perturbation theory gives a transition amplitude proportional to \((g/\hbar)\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{ivt^2/(2\hbar)}dt\). The magnitude of this Fresnel integral is \(\sqrt{2\pi\hbar/v}\), obtained by continuing the convergent Gaussian integral from a positive real damping parameter. The leading probability to change fixed basis states is therefore \(2\pi g^2/(\hbar v)\), valid only when small. The exact infinite linear-sweep Landau–Zener solution resums this dependence: the probability of retaining the initial diabatic label is \(\exp[-2\pi g^2/(\hbar v)]\). It refers to endpoints asymptotically far from the avoided crossing with constant coupling and no decoherence.

For \(g=0.5\) and \(v=1\) in units where \(\hbar=1\), the infinite-sweep retention probability is \(e^{-\pi/2}=0.2079\). Halving \(v\) squares it to 0.0432. The slow sweep therefore gives greater transfer between fixed basis states while preserving the lower-energy identity more faithfully. This distinction prevents reversing the interpretation of a measured probability.

Evolve a finite preparation numerically

Prepare the instantaneous ground state at detuning \(vt=-10\) and finish at \(+10\). Over each short step freeze \(H\) at its midpoint. Since \(H^2=h^2I\), the constant-step propagator is exactly \(\cos(h\Delta t)I-i\sin(h\Delta t)H/h\) in the chosen units. The product is unitary up to roundoff; finite time resolution still changes accumulated phases. Compare the population of the original fixed basis state with its population in the instantaneous ground state. Their separation through the crossing directly shows where ideal adiabatic following fails. The calculated final population can differ from the infinite-sweep expression because of endpoint mixing and phases.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on measurement choices

The coupling doubles while all else is fixed. How must the sweep rate change to leave the ideal infinite-sweep probability unchanged? Does a sweep over fixed detuning endpoints remain exactly equivalent?

Work through the solution

Keep \(g^2/v\) fixed by multiplying \(v\) by four. The infinite-sweep exponent is unchanged. At fixed endpoints the ratios \(|vt|/g\) at preparation and readout are now smaller, so the finite endpoints are less asymptotic. Their state mixing can change the observed result.

At zero detuning an energy measurement finds the lower eigenvalue with certainty. What would an immediate measurement in the fixed basis return?

Work through the solution

The lower eigenstate there is \((1,-1)/\sqrt2\) in the chosen convention. Each fixed-basis outcome therefore occurs with probability one half. Certainty in the instantaneous energy is compatible with maximal uncertainty concerning the diabatic label.

Further reading · Tokmakoff, time-dependent quantum mechanics and Landau–Zener transitions · Rubbmark and colleagues, experimental passage through an avoided crossing.

Problem sheet 7 · Measuring two phases with one interferometer

An ideal coherent interferometer has two paths for a charged spin-half particle. One loop encloses a magnetic flux. A controlled field direction can also rotate the spin adiabatically in one arm. Use independent calibrations of the flux and spin phase to predict what the recombiner measures. Assume identical path intensities, perfect coherence and matched dynamical phases.

Calibrate the magnetic flux from a ring spectrum

A separate narrow ring made for the same particle has \(E_n/E_L=(n-\varphi)^2\) with signed flux \(\varphi=q\Phi/h\). Near zero flux, the \(n=0\) energy is \(0.0625E_L\). An independent current measurement on that branch gives \(I/(qh/mL^2)=-0.25\). Find the flux and the nearest excited energy. Why are both observations useful?

Work through the solution

The energy gives \(|\varphi|=0.25\). The signed current is \(n-\varphi=-\varphi\) on this branch, so \(\varphi=+0.25\). The nearest excited branch is \(n=1\) at \(0.5625E_L\). The excitation gap is \(0.50E_L\). Energy alone leaves the sign undetermined.

Read off the energy branches and the signed ring current

Calibrate the spin phase by reversing a loop

Prepare the aligned spin state and carry the field direction once around a positive latitude at cone angle \(60^\circ\). Find the geometric phase for each traversal direction. With no magnetic flux, predict the bright-port probabilities at reference shifts \(\chi=0\) and \(\chi=+\pi/2\) for each direction.

Work through the solution

The solid angle is \(2\pi(1-\cos60^\circ)=\pi\), hence the forward phase is \(-\pi/2\) and the reverse phase \(+\pi/2\). In \(P=[1+\cos(\chi+\gamma)]/2\), zero reference phase gives one half for either traversal. A reference shift \(+\pi/2\) gives one for the forward loop and zero for its reverse. The second setting reveals the sign.

Reverse the latitude and read the two bright-port probabilities

Predict the combined measurement

In the interferometer the paths enclose the flux once with an orientation giving magnetic phase \(q\Phi/\hbar=2\pi\varphi\). At zero reference shift use the preceding calibrations to predict the bright-port probability with the forward spin loop and with its reversal. Then reverse the magnetic flux without changing the spin protocol.

Work through the solution

The magnetic phase is \(+\pi/2\), so the forward total is zero and the port is bright with probability one. The reverse spin loop gives total phase \(\pi\) and zero probability. Reversing the flux swaps those outcomes because the total phases become \(-\pi\) and zero. The two individual phases have different physical origins but their amplitudes multiply on the same path.

Check an imperfect observation

At the combined bright and dark settings an experiment instead records probabilities 0.91 and 0.09. Fit a visibility \(V\) in \(P=[1+V\cos\phi]/2\). Can these two points alone determine whether the loss was caused by dephasing or unequal path powers? Propose one independent observation.

Work through the solution

Their difference is \(V=0.82\) and their sum equals one, consistent with the balanced offset in the model. An incoherent admixture and unequal path amplitudes can both reduce the observed visibility. Measuring each arm intensity with the other arm blocked tests imbalance. Scanning the reference phase through a full period additionally checks whether a phase offset displaced the nominal extrema.

Problem sheet 8 · How a pair of neutral oscillators acquires an attractive interaction

Two neutral atoms in their ground states have zero permanent electric dipoles. A molecule nevertheless feels a long-range attraction to another neutral molecule. Treat one coordinate of each atom as a quantised oscillator. Solve the coupled pair twice and decide which parts of the result would survive in three dimensions.

First make a prediction at fixed separation

Two equal masses \(m\) have displacements \(x_1,x_2\) about fixed positive centres a distance \(R\) apart. In isolation both have angular frequency \(\omega_0\). The two induced electric moments are proportional to the displacements. The leading electrostatic interaction has energy \(g x_1x_2/R^3\), with orientation and charges absorbed into \(g\). Explain why a classical point resting at each minimum gains no energy, then ask what part of the Hamiltonian a quantum ground state would sample.

Work through the solution

Write \(k=m\omega_0^2\) and \(\lambda=g/(kR^3)\). Along \(x_2=0\) the potential rises as \(kx_1^2/2\). Along the diagonals \(x_1=\pm x_2\) it rises at different rates. For \(|\lambda|<1\) both slopes of restoring force are positive and the minimum remains at the origin with classical potential zero. The quantum oscillators have finite widths at zero temperature. Their ground state depends on both diagonal stiffnesses. A shift in that energy can occur even if the average position of each mass stays zero.

Diagonalise the complete Hamiltonian

Starting from kinetic energy \((p_1^2+p_2^2)/(2m)\), introduce normal coordinates and their conjugate momenta. Find both frequencies and the ground-state shift. Estimate it at \(\lambda=0.40\), in units of \(\hbar\omega_0\), sufficiently accurately to distinguish the exact result from its leading approximation.

Work through the solution

Use \(x_\pm=(x_1\pm x_2)/\sqrt2\) and the identical orthogonal transformation on momenta. Orthogonality preserves the kinetic energy and the canonical commutators. The product in the interaction is \((x_+^2-x_-^2)/2\). The two uncoupled Hamiltonians have spring constants \(k(1\pm\lambda)\). Each costs half a quantum in its own ground state. Subtract the energy of the two separated oscillators before comparing separations.

\[\omega_\pm=\omega_0\sqrt{1\pm\lambda},\]
\[\frac{\Delta E}{\hbar\omega_0}=\frac{\sqrt{1+\lambda}+\sqrt{1-\lambda}}2-1.\]

At 0.40 the two frequency ratios are 1.18322 and 0.77460. Their mean lies 0.02109 below one. The frequency that decreases loses more than the other gains because a square root is concave. Changing the sign of the coupling exchanges the normal coordinates and leaves the energy unchanged. This symmetry already excludes odd powers of the interaction.

Recover the same force from virtual transitions

Calculate the first two perturbative corrections in the basis of the isolated pair. Identify the intermediate state and its energy denominator. Obtain a power law for the force with the sign convention that increasing \(R\) is positive.

Work through the solution

Let \(x_0=\sqrt{\hbar/(2m\omega_0)}\). Acting on an isolated ground state, \(x\) creates one quantum with amplitude \(x_0\). The expectation of the interaction in \(|00\rangle\) vanishes because each coordinate has zero mean. One action of its product leaves only \(|11\rangle\). The matrix element is \(k\lambda x_0^2=\lambda\hbar\omega_0/2\), and the excited state lies two quanta above the ground state. The denominator \(E_{00}-E_{11}\) is therefore negative.

\[\Delta E^{(2)}=-\frac{\hbar\omega_0}{8}\lambda^2=-\frac{C_6}{R^6},\]
\[C_6=\frac{\hbar\omega_0g^2}{8k^2},\]
\[F_R=-\frac{6C_6}{R^7}.\]

At the specified coupling the quadratic approximation gives −0.02000. Expanding each square root and adding yields the next correction \(-5\lambda^4/128\). At 0.40 that term is −0.00100. The formal expansion parameter is a stiffness ratio. Calling the interaction weak requires a separation large enough to make this ratio small, even if each microscopic charge is individually large.

What would distinguish the power law in data?

Use separation units \(R_0\) and set the coupling there to 0.40. Before opening the graph, predict the factor by which the leading energy shift changes at \(2R_0\). Is the corresponding factor the same for force? Then change the coupling at the reference separation. Compare the exact and perturbative curves where the soft frequency approaches zero.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Work through the solution

Doubling the separation divides the coupling by eight. The leading energy falls in magnitude by \(8^2=64\), and the force by 128. For the reference coupling the exact shifts at \(R_0\) and \(2R_0\) have magnitudes about 0.02109 and 0.000313 in units of one quantum. The ratio of exact energies is about 67.4. A fit near strong coupling therefore gives a separation exponent steeper than six. Measuring it does not establish a new long-distance law. Repeat farther apart and check whether the estimate approaches six.

A mode becoming soft signals the breakdown of the quadratic model. At small separation real atoms overlap and exchange matters. At distances comparable with a transition wavelength, electromagnetic propagation time matters. This two-coordinate electrostatic model predicts neither limit. Extending it to three dimensions also requires carrying the dipole orientation tensor through the intermediate states. The coefficient found here is specific to the stated geometry.

Follow the independent development of stationary perturbation theory in the MIT Quantum Physics III course material.

Apply a translation operator, reduce a many-site Hamiltonian to a small matrix and use the resulting eigenvectors to predict an edge state. Compare a band touching with a gapped system.

Translational symmetry and diffraction

Condensed matter and band theory

Work backwards from a scattering pattern to the repeating translations and the atoms within one cell. Learn which structural information an intensity measurement loses.

From first principles

Recover a lattice spacing from phase addition

Illuminate a row of \(N\) equally spaced atoms with a coherent plane wave and observe weak elastic scattering far away. Each atom sees an incident phase and contributes an outgoing phase. Their difference depends on \(\mathbf q=\mathbf k_{\rm out}-\mathbf k_{\rm in}\). With the origin at the first atom, let \(\phi=\mathbf q\cdot\mathbf a\) be the phase difference between neighbours displaced by \(\mathbf a\). Up to an overall sign convention the total amplitude is a geometric sum:

\[A=f\sum_{j=0}^{N-1} e^{ij\phi}=f\,\frac{1-e^{iN\phi}}{1-e^{i\phi}} .\]

Here \(f\) is the amplitude from one atom. Use \(|1-e^{ix}|=2|\sin(x/2)|\) and take the squared magnitude. When \(\phi=2\pi m\), evaluate the original sum directly:

\[I\propto |f|^2\frac{\sin^2(N\phi/2)}{\sin^2(\phi/2)},\]
\[I_{\rm peak}\propto N^2|f|^2 .\]

For a three-dimensional lattice every primitive translation must meet this phase condition. The resulting peak positions identify reciprocal-lattice vectors. For several atoms in each cell the single-cell factor becomes \(\sum_s f_s e^{i\mathbf q\cdot\mathbf r_s}\). Its squared magnitude can vanish even at a permitted reciprocal vector. An absent peak can therefore reveal interference inside the cell.

Derivation and applications

Extract atomic displacement from the loss of Bragg intensity

A lattice can keep the same mean spacing while its Bragg peaks lose intensity on heating. Consider a crystal of identical atoms whose displacements about their mean positions fluctuate during the exposure. Treat scattering in the single-scattering approximation. At a reciprocal vector of magnitude \(Q\), every mean lattice position supplies the same phase. The instantaneous displacement projected along the scattering vector, \(u\), adds a phase \(Qu\). If this component is Gaussian with mean zero and variance \(s^2\), averaging the complex amplitude gives

\[\left\langle e^{iQu}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi s^2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-u^2/(2s^2)}e^{iQu}\,du=e^{-Q^2s^2/2}.\]

The last equality follows by Fourier transformation of the normalized Gaussian. The coherent peak measures the square of this average amplitude. Squaring the instantaneous phase factor first would give unity and erase the effect of phase differences between sites. With \(I_0(Q)\) the coherent intensity of the perfectly stationary array, the Debye–Waller attenuation is

\[I_{\rm Bragg}(Q)=I_0(Q)e^{-Q^2s^2}.\]

The formula describes the sharp coherent contribution. The difference between the total averaged intensity and the coherent intensity appears in diffuse scattering. Stronger attenuation at large \(Q\) follows because an identical displacement spans a larger fraction of a scattering phase cycle. For corrected integrated intensities of the same reflection at two temperatures, taken at essentially the same \(Q\), the unknown stationary intensity cancels,

\[s_2^2-s_1^2=-\frac{\ln[I_2(Q)/I_1(Q)]}{Q^2}.\]

This comparison assumes unchanged occupancies, instrument normalization and negligible changes in the mean structure. For isotropic displacement \(s^2\) is one third of the mean squared three-dimensional displacement.

Worked example

Recover a change in mean-square displacement

A reflection at \(Q=50.0\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\) falls from 10,000 to 8,000 corrected counts. Its projected variance increases by \(-\ln(0.800)/2500=8.93\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{nm^2}\). If its original projected root-mean-square displacement was \(0.00600\,\mathrm{nm}\), the new value is \(\sqrt{0.00600^2+0.0000893}=0.0112\,\mathrm{nm}\). Adding the displacements directly would use the wrong measured quantity.

Second application

Test the angular dependence with another reflection

A second specimen retains 90.0 percent of its low-temperature peak intensity at \(Q=30.0\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\). For the same displacement direction at twice that wavevector, the attenuation exponent quadruples. The predicted retention is \(0.900^4=0.656\). A corrected retention near 0.90 for both reflections would instead suggest a common change in illuminated volume or flux that this displacement model cannot explain.

What repeats in a crystal?

A one-dimensional chain with identical atoms every \(a\) has translations \(R=na\). Replacing every other atom by a different species doubles the primitive translation, even if the nearest-neighbour separation stays fixed. The set of equivalent cell origins is the Bravais lattice; the atoms placed relative to each origin form the basis. Choosing a larger unit cell is allowed but folds distinct waves onto extra branches. Count independent degrees of freedom per unit length as a check on any cell choice.

In three dimensions write \(\mathbf R=\sum_i n_i\mathbf a_i\). Define reciprocal vectors by \(\mathbf a_i\cdot\mathbf b_j=2\pi\delta_{ij}\). Then \(\mathbf G=\sum_i h_i\mathbf b_i\) has \(e^{i\mathbf G\cdot\mathbf R}=1\) at every equivalent origin. These vectors have units of inverse length. For a rectangular lattice doubling a real-space period halves the corresponding reciprocal spacing. The first Brillouin zone consists of wavevectors closer to the reciprocal origin than to any other reciprocal point. Later we will label collective vibrations and electronic states inside this region.

Construct reciprocal space from the primitive cell

A diffraction experiment measures momentum transfer. To predict its possible values, ask which plane waves acquire the same phase at every translated lattice point. For a Bravais lattice with translations \(\mathbf R=\sum_i n_i\mathbf a_i\), the condition is \(\exp(i\mathbf G\cdot\mathbf R)=1\) for every integer choice of \(n_i\). It suffices to require \(\mathbf G\cdot\mathbf a_i=2\pi h_i\). A reciprocal basis therefore satisfies \(\mathbf a_i\cdot\mathbf b_j=2\pi\delta_{ij}\) and generates all such vectors by integer combinations. In matrix form, if \(A\) holds the primitive vectors as columns, then \(B=2\pi(A^{-1})^{\mathsf T}\). The transpose matters whenever the original axes are oblique.

For a triangular layer take \(\mathbf a_1=(a,0)\) and \(\mathbf a_2=(a/2,\sqrt3a/2)\). The constraints on \(\mathbf b_1\) first give \(b_{1x}=2\pi/a\) and then \(b_{1y}=-2\pi/(\sqrt3a)\). Similarly \(\mathbf b_2=(0,4\pi/(\sqrt3a))\). Verify that translating along \(\mathbf a_2\) leaves the phase of \(\mathbf b_1\) unchanged. Reciprocal vectors have units of inverse length.

How far can a wave vector move from the origin before an equivalent representative is nearer? The boundary shared with \(\mathbf G\) solves \(|\mathbf k|=|\mathbf k-\mathbf G|\), or \(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf G=|\mathbf G|^2/2\). The half-planes around the origin define the first Brillouin zone. Here the six shortest reciprocal vectors give a hexagon. With \(a=0.300\,\mathrm{nm}\) the shortest \(|\mathbf G|\) is \(4\pi/(\sqrt3 a)=24.18\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\), putting a face midpoint \(12.09\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\) from the centre. The zone area is \((2\pi)^2/(\sqrt3a^2/2)=506.5\,\mathrm{nm^{-2}}\), equal to one reciprocal primitive-cell area. Doubling all lattice lengths halves the distances in the diffraction pattern and quarters its zone area. These reciprocal positions follow from translation symmetry. The measured intensities also depend on what occupies each cell.

Add amplitudes before taking intensities

An elastic plane wave scattered from positions \(na+d_j\) accumulates a phase equal to the momentum-transfer wavevector \(Q\) times position. For \(N\) identical cells the amplitude factorises:

\[A(Q)=\underbrace{\sum_j f_j(Q)e^{iQd_j}}_{F(Q)}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}e^{iQna},\qquad I(Q)\propto |A(Q)|^2 .\]

The last sum is geometric. Its magnitude is \(|\sin(NQa/2)/\sin(Qa/2)|\), with limiting value \(N\) at \(Qa=2\pi h\). Large finite crystals have narrow peaks of height proportional to \(N^2\). The separation to the adjacent first zero is \(2\pi/(Na)\); doubling coherent length halves this width. The basis factor \(F\) sets the relative integrated strengths for a given lattice. A finite instrument resolution also broadens measured peaks and should be characterised before inferring a sample size.

An extinction is evidence about the basis

Place two sites at \(d=0,a/2\), with real scattering amplitudes \(f_A=3\) and \(f_B=1\) held constant over the angular range. At \(G_h=2\pi h/a\), \(F_h=3+(-1)^h\). Even and odd orders have intensities proportional to 16 and 4 respectively, before angular or instrumental corrections. If both amplitudes become equal, the odd peaks vanish. If the two sites have identical chemistry as well as identical positions up to translation, a cell of length \(a/2\) would describe the structure more economically. Equal scattering amplitudes in a particular probe alone cannot establish chemical identity.

For incident wavelength \(\lambda\), with the total scattering angle \(2\theta\), \(|Q|=(4\pi/\lambda)\sin\theta\). At \(\lambda=0.154\) nm and spacing \(d=0.200\) nm the first Bragg peak therefore occurs at \(\theta=22.6^\circ\), or detector angle \(45.3^\circ\). A factor-of-two error in reading the detector would corrupt any inferred lattice constant.

What survives a change of origin?

Translating the entire basis by \(d_0\) multiplies \(A(Q)\) by \(e^{iQd_0}\). Intensity is unchanged, so diffraction cannot locate the absolute origin. More generally the phase of the amplitude is missing from a measurement of \(|A|^2\). Peak positions suggest candidate lattices, corrected intensities test a proposed basis and peak widths probe finite coherence or strain. These are different questions even when addressed with the same scan.

Derive the selection rules of a cubic powder pattern

A powdered crystal supplies many crystallite orientations, so a fixed detector angle can intercept every family of reciprocal planes with the appropriate spacing. For a cubic lattice with conventional cell parameter \(a\), reciprocal vectors have magnitude \(2\pi\sqrt{h^2+k^2+l^2}/a\). Bragg's condition then gives \(\sin^2\theta=\lambda^2(h^2+k^2+l^2)/(4a^2)\). The sequence of squared sines can be used to index the pattern, but only reciprocal points with nonzero structure amplitude produce peaks.

For a monatomic face-centred cubic crystal, describe the lattice with conventional cubic coordinates and basis positions \((0,0,0)\), \((0,\tfrac12,\tfrac12)\), \((\tfrac12,0,\tfrac12)\) and \((\tfrac12,\tfrac12,0)\). For identical atomic amplitude \(f\) their phase sum is

\[F_{hkl}=f[1+(-1)^{k+l}+(-1)^{h+l}+(-1)^{h+k}] .\]

If all indices are even or all are odd, every phase factor is positive and \(F=4f\). Mixed parity makes the sum zero. Thus the first allowed squared-index sums are 3 for (111), 4 for (200), 8 for (220), 11 for (311) and 12 for (222). These extinctions encode the centring translations; adding an internal basis to each lattice site can introduce additional missing peaks. Powder intensity also depends on multiplicity and on angle-dependent scattering factors, so the strongest peak need not have the smallest indices.

Infer a lattice parameter and a strain

Suppose powder peaks under wavelength 0.1540 nm occur at detector angles \(2\theta=38.48^\circ\), \(44.74^\circ\) and \(65.10^\circ\). Their squared half-angle sines are about 0.1086, 0.1448 and 0.2896. After division by the first they give \(1:1.33:2.67\), consistent with \(3:4:8\). Indexing the middle peak as (200) yields \(a=\lambda/\sin22.37^\circ=0.405\) nm. The other two peaks independently give the same value to the displayed precision, unlike an arbitrary assignment to successive integers.

Now let the (200) detector angle fall by \(0.100^\circ\) under load. Differentiating \(2d\sin\theta=\lambda\) gives \(\Delta d/d\simeq-\cot\theta\,\Delta\theta\). Here \(\Delta\theta=-0.0500^\circ=-8.73\times10^{-4}\) rad, hence \(\Delta d/d=+2.12\times10^{-3}\). That is tensile strain along the scattering vector. A single peak shift cannot establish an isotropic volume strain without further orientations. Multiple peaks can also separate a uniform scale error in wavelength from a genuine directional strain. In the unstrained limit the lattice parameter extracted independently from every correctly indexed family should agree within instrumental uncertainty.

Peak position and width respond differently to imperfections. If all sampled crystallites undergo the same fractional change of their plane spacing, their peaks shift together by the differential Bragg relation. A distribution of tensile and compressive strains can instead broaden the peak around an almost unchanged mean angle. A finite crystallite size can also broaden it, but its angular dependence across several reflection families differs from the leading strain broadening. Comparing several indexed peaks therefore gives more information than treating a wide single line as direct evidence for a particular mean lattice parameter.

Instrumental angle calibration should be checked with a reference powder so that a detector zero shift does not masquerade as a specimen strain.

Tell a finite-size minimum from a basis extinction

Eight identical cells contain sites at 0 and half a lattice period. Sum the eight cell amplitudes coherently, then multiply by the basis amplitude \(f_A+f_B e^{iQa/2}\). Here the form factors are real, positive and independent of angle.
